Automorphic Symmetries, String integrable structures and Deformations
Anton Pribytok

Abstract
We address the novel structures arising in quantum and string integrable theories, as well as construct methods to obtain them and provide further analysis. Specifically, we implement the automorphic symmetries on periodic lattice systems for obtaining integrable hierarchies, whose commutativity along with integrable transformations induces a generating structure of integrable classes. This prescription is first applied to 2-dim and 4-dim setups, where we find the new 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 \mathfrak{sl}_{2} sector, 𝔰 ​ 𝔲 ​ ( 2 ) ⊕ 𝔰 ​ 𝔲 ​ ( 2 ) \mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2) with superconductive modes, Generalised Hubbard type classes and more. The corresponding 2- and 4-dim R R matrices are resolved through perturbation theory, that allows to recover an exact result. We then construct a boost recursion that allows to address the systems, whose R R -/ S S -matrices exhibit arbitrary spectral dependence, that also is an apparent property of the scattering operators in A ​ d ​ S AdS integrability. It is then possible to implement the last for Hamiltonian Ansätze in D = 2 , 3 , 4 D=2,3,4 , which leads to new models in all dimensions. We also provide a method based on a coupled differential system that allows to resolve for R R matrices exactly. Importantly, one can isolate a special class of models of non-difference form in 2-dim case (6vB/8vB), which provides a new structure consistently arising in A ​ d ​ S 3 AdS_{3} and A ​ d ​ S 2 AdS_{2} string backgrounds. We prove that these classes can be represented as deformations of the A ​ d ​ S { 2 , 3 } AdS_{\{2,3\}} models. We also work out that the latter satisfy free fermion constraint, braiding unitarity, crossing and exhibit deformed algebraic structure that shares certain properties with A ​ d ​ S 3 × S 3 × ℳ 4 AdS_{3}\times S^{3}\times\mathcal{M}^{4} and A ​ d ​ S 2 × S 2 × T 6 AdS_{2}\times S^{2}\times T^{6} models. The embedding and mappings of known A ​ d ​ S { 2 , 3 } AdS_{\{2,3\}} models to 6vB/8vB deformations are demonstrated, along with a discussion on the associated candidates of sigma models.



School of Mathematics
& Hamilton Mathematics Institute

Trinity College Dublin

Dublin, Ireland

1
Summary

The structure of present dissertation is given as follows, we start by introducing the necessary objects, apparatus and properties that will underlie the essence of the questions we raise,
e.g.
finding the novel integrable structures in various dimensions, the obstacles arising in the classification program, development of a novel method for the latter, identification of the properties, relations to other integrable sectors and many more
2
.

First of all, we develop a method based on automorphic boost symmetries, which allows to investigate the existence of a new integrable sector in two-dimensional lattice systems. To achieve that, we consider a generic Hamiltonian Ansatz for a spin chain with 2-dim local space
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. We implement the boost automorphism for closed spin chains and generate the local charges, whose commutativity guarantees integrability. The solution space that follows from resolving a polynomial system of the commutativity constraint requires a separate treatment for inducing a structure on it. This can be achieved by the integrable identification transformations, which isolate associated solution generators, where every such generator defines a class. Then the corresponding
R
matrices are worked out by a novel perturbative bottom-up approach along with all quantum consistency checks. In this base setting, not only a full known model classification is achieved, but also novel integrable structures are found in the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
sector, whose underlying quantum algebra still remains to be resolved. In addition, the applied technique provided completion for the
\mathbb{C}^{1|1}
sector and established graded mapping, Sec.
3
.

The four-dimensional analysis followed from the boost method discovered in the first part. The Hubbard symmetry algebra has been taken as a base for constructing Hamiltonian Ansätze in 4-dim. By appropriate extensions of the approach, 8 new integrable classes have been found, the five of which followed from distinct representations of
\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)
and the rest three came from the so called Generalised Hubbard Models. The GHM sector was obtained from the separate construction, that contained Hubbard kinetic part, generilised hopping, flipping and potential terms up to quadruple order in number operators. A separate method based on
\mathcal{H}
-
R
differential system was proposed and proven to find all the underlying
R
matrices satisfying all consistency conditions. The discussion on resolution of the new models through ABA and QSC is provided, Sec.
4
.

At the next stage, a generalisation to higher dimensions and arbitrary spectral dependence is considered, Sec.
5
. Namely, one can address the charge
\mathcal{Q}
automorphic recursion for the systems, whose
R
-/
S
-matrix exhibits non-difference (non-additive) spectral dependence, which is characteristic for an integrable model classes arising in
AdS_{n}
backgrounds. In what we derive the boost generator that respects arbitrary spectral dependence and seek for the new structures in
D=2,3,4
. In two dimensions four new models found that are relevant for the
AdS_{\{2,3\}}
integrable string backgrounds. The 3-dim sector contains nontrivial pseudo-difference models exceptionally in the sub-15-vertex classes. In dimension four, we find a perturbative support for a new structure to exist in the q-deformed sector of
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
. It have been also noted that some of the difference form models do not induce the non-difference form analogues. A comparison analysis of formalism with both spectral dependence types is performed. The
R
-sector is resolved by constructing an approach of coupled Sutherland type system, that leads to non-linear differential system, solution of which establishes exact
R
matrices.

Consequently the 2-dim sector of arbitrary spectral form (or pseudo-difference) is addressed for its properties and algebraic structure. Specifically, it is 6- and 8-vertex models, that are identified as B class that are relevant for
AdS_{3}
and
AdS_{2}
integrable deformations, Sec.
6
,
7
. In the first part we prove that these models satisfy the free-fermion condition, braiding unitarity and crossing symmetry. It is shown that free fermions provide useful constraints for modified ABA of
AdS
models with background fluxes. In the last part we prove an algebra of 6vB and 8vB arise in deformed form w.r.t.
AdS_{3}\times S^{3}\times\mathcal{M}^{4}
and
AdS_{2}\times S^{2}\times T^{6}
models. The 6vB/8vB were established as
AdS_{\{2,3\}}
deformations, in this respect, the corresponding mappings and embeddings of known
AdS
models along with their deformations have been resolved.

2
Introduction

It is known that many interacting field theoretic setups exhibit number important physical properties, where at the same time computation of their specific observables involves resolution of infinities a.k.a. different regularisation schemes and additional technical tools. One of the ways to regulate classes of field theoretic models is the spatial discretisation. This procedure allows to bring field (continuous) models to finite-volume models with finite degrees of freedom (dof). Another important characteristic of a class of field theory models is
integrability
, which initially stemmed from the classical notion of being (infinite-dimensional) Hamiltonian integrable system
[
1
]
, and later quantum which exhibited (in)finite conserved quantum symmetries
[
2
]
, that in turn, define complete
exact solvability
of such models through a variety of techniques. Combining both notions of integrability and space discreteness we obtain a very rich class of integrable models and techniques deriving from them.

2.1
Automorphism generators and charges

It is well known there exists a variety of quantum field and statistical theories that respect factorised scattering in one or the other form. The fundamental characteristic of the last is an infinite tower of conservation laws (quantities), which makes them exactly solvable. It can be also established that if an
R
- or
S
-matrix accordingly satisfy YB constraint (
2.23
)

S_{12}S_{13}S_{23}=S_{23}S_{13}S_{12}\qquad S_{ij}\in\text{End}\left(\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\right)

(2.1)

then the corresponding transfer matrix
t(x)

t(u)=\text{Tr}_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{L}S_{0i}\qquad t(u)t(v)=\text{Tr}_{a}\text{Tr}_{b}\,\prod_{i=1}^{L}\left[S_{ai}(u)S_{bi}(v)\right],

(2.2)

As indicated in (
2.2
), the trace is taken over the product of
S
-operators (
S
-monodromy), which describe scattering of particles of various types Fig.
1
. The commutativity of
t
implies its diagonalisation through known integrability methods
[
3
,
4
]
.

Figure 1:
Auxiliary null-space tracing of
S
with particle species
m_{k}
and
n_{k}

In general for the quantum transfer matrix
t
to exist, there must exist a conserved quantum monodromy based on asymptotic particle states, which satisfies
RTT
-factorisation and the discrete analogues of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
can be realised in the underlying quantum theory. Given prescription along with unitarity relations proposed in
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
]
is taken to be conventional, since it also permits to construct the corresponding conserved charges by perturbing (
2.2
) and obtain induced commutativity
[
3
,
10
]
.

Remarkably, from YB relations one can establish a group of transformations
\mathcal{G}_{\bar{L}}
on the hierarchy of conserved charges, which can be proven to constitute the discrete analogue of the Lorentz symmetry. Indeed this group of continuous transformations analogous to the Lorentzian one has direct implications for integrable systems on the lattice. In fact, the Lorentz boosts identify the set of transformations on the pairwise commuting integrals of motion (conserved charges, including Hamiltonian). It is also possible to consider a continuous limit of this lattice Lorentz group, in which case it can decomposed into Lorentz group and a group of transformations of higher integrals of motion
[
11
]
. Specifically, one can also consider parametric series of the given lattice group, where infinite tower of boost generators is mapped to an infinite set of motion integrals.

We can also note this from the continuous setting. If we consider the Lorentz transformation of a certain generating and conserved observable, like a current
\mathcal{J}^{\mu}(x)
of the underlying quantum theory

\mathcal{U}\mathcal{J}^{\mu}(x)\mathcal{U}^{-1}=\Lambda_{\nu}^{\mu}\,\mathcal{J}^{\nu}(\Lambda x)

(2.3)

where
\mathcal{U}\left[\Lambda(u)\right]
and
\Lambda_{\mu}^{\nu}
is Lorentz operator with fugacity u. In addition, the Lorentzian limit defines the boost
\mathcal{B}
and its commutation properties provide an (integrable) algebraic structure

\mathcal{B}=\dd_{\,u}\Lambda(u)\big|_{u=0}\qquad\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{J}^{\mu}_{a}(x)\right]=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,x^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\mathcal{J}^{\mu}_{a}(x)+\epsilon^{\mu\beta}\mathcal{J}_{a,\,\beta}^{\mu}

(2.4)

where
a
indicates symmetry indices. By looking at the commutation (
2.4
), we can get commutativity with the charge
[
12
]

\left[\mathcal{B},Q_{a}\right]=0\qquad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{J}_{a}(\pm\infty)\equiv 0,\quad Q_{a}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\dd\,x\mathcal{J}_{a}^{0}(x)

(2.5)

In fact one could achieve an equivalent result with higher currents (accounting for quantum corrections) by looking at commutativity with the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
of the system
[
13
]
. It can be immediately seen that the above mentioned decomposition and higher symmetries are reflected not only at the level of the Poincaré algebra, but would also admit infinite-dimensional extension of internal symmetries, where the boost manifests its automorphic nature,
i.e.

\text{Poincar\'{e}:}\qquad\left[\mathcal{P}_{-},\mathcal{P}_{+}\right]=0,\quad\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\right]=\pm\mathcal{P}_{\pm}

(2.6)

\text{Yangian supplement }\mathcal{Y}\left[\mathfrak{g}\right]\text{: }\quad\begin{cases}\quad\left[\mathcal{P}_{\pm},Q_{a}\right]=0\\
\quad\left[\mathcal{B},Q_{a}\right]=0\\
\end{cases}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\quad\left[\mathcal{P}_{\pm},\,^{(1)}Q_{a}\right]=0\\
\quad\left[\mathcal{B},\,^{(1)}Q_{a}\right]=-\mathfrak{C}\dfrac{\hbar}{4\pi i}Q_{a}\\
\end{array}

(2.7)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\pm}
are lightcone translations,
\mathfrak{C}
is the Casimir of the enveloping algebra and by
{}^{(n)}Q_{a}
we shall indicate level-
n
Yangian generators (internal generating symmetry). We can notice that the boost
\mathcal{B}
generates nontrivial internal symmetry extension (
2.34
)-(
2.37
).

As we shall see further, this formalism can be translated into discrete case upon appropriate structure resolutions and conditions. However it is also important to note that boost construction (
2.4
) is characteristic for continuous systems and is distinctive from the boost automorphism for integrable systems in discretised space. Moreover for a number of integrable models it is not clear if there exist mapping limits (
e.g.
irrelevantly deformed 2-dim CFTs
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
). Particularly, it would allow to identify novel integrable structures and provide full control over symmetries. Let us now analyse in greater detail how to implement the automorphic apparatus and what are implications of the above conserved quantities for lattice systems.

2.2
Quantum system on the lattice and Master Symmetries

Despite the fact that field theories exhibit very distinct structure, properties and interpretation, it is important to acquire maps from continuous symmetries to their discrete analogues in
1+1
-dim. Indeed, by construction the symmetry algebra above along with
\mathcal{Y}\left[\mathfrak{g}\right]
internal extension, possesses compatible discretised structure in the Hopf realisation (algebraic actions, quotients)
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
. Moreover all operations can be straightforwardly replaced by their discrete counterparts (space stratification, locality, summation etc). Importantly as we shall show below, these discontinuous objects must also close to a symmetry algebra.

Such integrable systems are defined on the discretised spaces or
lattices
, hence the name of the class -
lattice integrable models
. In particular, by lattice models we shall mean Integrable Spin Chains (ISC), which are quantum spin models of diverse group-theoretic and physical properties. The canonical definition of the spin chain includes the collection of lattice sites assembled into spin chain of length
L
, with local quantum space
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
assigned to each site, hence the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}_{L}
of the system

\mathfrak{H}_{L}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{L}\mathfrak{h}_{i}

(2.8)

where a variety of spin chain systems can include different dimensionality and representations.

Figure 2:
Heisenberg spin chain (magnet class) with Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
formed by local quantum space
\mathfrak{h}
(spins) at site
i
.

Specifically at each local space
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
there is some spin orientation
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
, which forms a state in the system

\ket{\psi_{s}}=\ket{\cdots\mathfrak{s}_{i}\,\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}\,\mathfrak{s}_{i+2}\cdots}\quad\qquad\ket{\psi_{s}}\in\mathcal{H},\,\,\mathfrak{s}_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}

(2.9)

where
\mathfrak{s}_{k}
corresponds to a local basis vector assigned to
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
. It can be clearly illustrated by the example of a spin chain with two-dimensional local quantum space
\mathfrak{h}_{i}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Specifically, one can define generic spin-
\dfrac{1}{2}
Heisenberg Hamiltonian of the system in an external magnetic field

H_{XYZ}^{\varkappa}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{L}J_{x}\sigma_{i}^{x}\sigma_{i+1}^{x}+J_{y}\sigma_{i}^{y}\sigma_{i+1}^{y}+J_{z}\sigma_{i}^{z}\sigma_{i+1}^{z}+\varkappa\sigma_{i}^{z}

(2.10)

where
J_{i}
are components of a coupling constant, Pauli matrices
\sigma_{i}
and external field
\varkappa
. In fact, we shall be considering L-site closed spin chains with quantum spin-operators
S_{i}
that obey periodicity and algebra on it
A.1

S_{L+1}^{\mu}\equiv S_{1}^{\mu}\qquad\left[S_{j}^{\mu},S_{k}^{\nu}\right]=i\delta_{jk}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}S_{j}^{\rho}

(2.11)

where for spin-
\dfrac{1}{2}
case
S_{j}^{\mu}
are Pauli operators
S_{j}^{\mu}=\dfrac{\sigma_{j}^{\mu}}{2}
. One can further proceed with the set of reduced models, which are obtained through identifications of the coupling of the so called XYZ-model from above. Namely, for
J_{y}=J_{x}
one obtains XXZ-model or the XXX-model for
J_{x}=J_{y}=J_{z}=J
. XYZ, XXZ and XXX-model are characterised by Jacobi elliptic, trigonometric and rational function, hence the name of the functional sectors they define. It is possible to reduce even further by removing interaction along specific directions, for instance one can get XY-model for
J_{z}=0
or quantum Ising chain in the transversal field with
J_{y}=J_{z}=0
.

Figure 3:
Distinct boundary conditions correspond to different types of integrable spin chains. Here

constitute specific boundary and

is
n
-dimensional local quantum space at site
i
.

It is also important to distinguish between types of the spin chains depending on their boundary condition Fig.

spin chain:
-\infty\leftarrow\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{1}
,

•

,

•

spin chain:
\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{1}=V_{0}

\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{2}=V_{L+1}

•

(0)\rightarrow(L+1),\,\,\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{1}=\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{2}=V_{L+1}

•

Cyclic
spin chain:
\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{1}=\partial\mathrm{\Sigma}_{2}=V_{L+1}
along with shift symmetry
1

1

in general the level-one Yangian does not commute with shift operator
\left[\mathcal{U},^{(1)}\mathcal{Y}[J_{a}]\right]\neq 0

i\rightarrow i\pm 1
, and the total momentum of the spin chain excitations vanishes
P=0
.

As it was stated above, the central characteristics of an integrable spin chain are symmetries and associated conserved charges, which can be represented in the hierarchy set
\{\mathbb{Q}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}\dots\mathbb{Q}_{r}\dots\mathbb{Q}_{n}\}
and completely define the underlying integrable system. In the context of spin chains the first two charges are usually assigned to translations/momentum
\mathbb{Q}_{1}\equiv P
and Hamiltonian
\mathbb{Q}_{2}\equiv\mathbb{H}
. For the case of a spin chain with interacting adjacent sites, it is identified as a chain with the
nearest-neighbour
(NN) interaction. In the present framework,
\mathbb{H}
is exactly an operator that defines NN-interactions. So the charge
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
that spans
r
-sites constitutes an interaction range
r
. It is sometimes referred to as higher charge Fig.
4
(or
higher rank Hamiltonians
as it will become clear below). It means that on the spin chain we can define local densities of the appropriate operators, the sum of which will correspond to the full operator on the given spin chain

Figure 4:
Local charge densities acting on the spin chain

\mathbb{Q}_{2}=\mathbb{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\mathcal{H}_{i,i+1}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{H}_{L,L+1}=\mathcal{H}_{L,1}

(2.12)

with Hamiltonian density
\mathcal{H}
at
\left(i,i+1\right)
-sites and the spin chain periodicity (closure condition). So in general, the core integrable structure is encoded in mutual commutativity of the above

\left[\mathbb{Q}_{r},\mathbb{Q}_{s}\right]=0\qquad\mathbb{Q}_{x}\equiv\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathcal{Q}_{k,\,k+1,\,\dots\,,\,k+x-1}

(2.13)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
indicate range
x
charge. Although we need to remark that such labelling is not always possible. It is a class of spin chains that produces such consistent mapping. It will be explained that automorphism generating property and periodic boundary conditions are important for this. Characteristic properties of these charges are locality and homogeneity. Namely, the
\mathcal{Q}_{x}
densities act locally on a given number of spins
x
, interaction is isotropic w.r.t. all sites and all charges respect a group
G
symmetry with an underlying algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. However by implementing the boost construction from above, one still needs an additional object to strictly prove integrability and uniqueness of the novel class.

Such a fundamental object turns out to carry scattering data, braiding symmetry and subsequently the spectrum generating information for the quantum integrable model – quantum
R
-matrix. For the construction, it is necessary to consider a vector space
\mathbb{V}
over field
\mathfrak{F}
and a linear map

R:\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}

(2.14)

with endomorphic structure on
R_{ij}\in\text{End}\left(\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\right)
, where appropriate continuation on identities is assumed

R_{12}=R\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}}\qquad R_{23}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}}\otimes R\qquad R_{13}=\left(\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}}\otimes\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}}\right).\left(R\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}}\right).\left(\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{V}}\otimes\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}}\right)

(2.15)

with twist map
2
2

2

which in the integrable lattice systems corresponds to permutation operator
P_{ij}

\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}}
defined as follows

\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}}:\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\qquad\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}}\left[\mathbb{W}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{W}_{2}\right]\equiv\mathbb{W}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{W}_{1}

(2.16)

Structure and properties of the quantum
R
-matrix are completely identified by the main quantum constraint (or more specifically, characterised by the underlying quantum group structure). The study of braid groups, quantum symmetries and algebra of quantum operators leads us to the main constraint – Quantum Yang-Baxter equation (QYBE)
5

R_{12}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)R_{13}\left(z_{1},z_{3}\right)R_{23}\left(z_{2},z_{3}\right)=R_{23}\left(z_{2},z_{3}\right)R_{13}\left(z_{1},z_{3}\right)R_{12}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)

(2.17)

with
z_{i}
to be a spectral parameter associated to space
i
. In addition, the
R
-matrix possesses special spectral points, and in particular, reduces to the permutation operator for coinciding parameters

R_{12}(z,z)=P_{12}

(2.18)

which corresponds to the regularity condition. It can be also shown that the Hamiltonian is obtained from
\log
-derivative of the
R
-matrix
A.1

\mathbb{H}_{12}=R_{12}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)^{-1}\partial_{z_{1}}R_{12}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)\big|_{z_{2}=z_{1}=z}=P_{12}\partial_{z_{1}}R_{12}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)\big|_{z_{2}=z_{1}=z}

(2.19)

It is important that for a large class of integrable models it is possible to obtain reduced spectral dependence of the underlying
R
-matrix. As we shall see, that will result in significant computational reductions, analytic properties and form of the derived operators of the new models. Generically, one can implement the quantum

-matrix in the fundamental
\mathbf{GL}\left(N,\mathbb{C}\right)
representation
R_{12}^{*}\left(z\right)\in\text{Mat}\left(N,\mathbb{C}\right)^{\otimes 2}

R_{12}^{*}\left(z\right)=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{N}R_{ijkl}^{*}\left(z\right)E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}

(2.20)

where
R_{12}^{*}\left(z\right)
is
N^{2}\times N^{2}
matrix, asterisk indicates presence of a quantum or other characteristic parameter (e.g.
\hbar
),

is a spectral parameter and

constitutes a basis in
\text{Mat}\left(N,\mathbb{C}\right)
.

or reassociating

One of the questions that will be addressed throughout this work is the classification of the quantum integrable models, which from many perspectives is a complex problem on its own. For example, a number of attempts for new integrable models, were based on the resolvability of the Yang-Baxter equation (
2.17
) or (
2.24
), which apriori is not a straightforward task. Moreover, it is known to be directly solvable only for certain subsectors
[
22
]
. A subsector of integrable lattice vertex models and their relation with Zamolodchikov tetrahedral algebra, as well as constant YBE solutions were shown in
[

,

,

,

. More recently a classification of
\{4\text{-}8\}
-vertex via differential approach was given in
[

,
28
]
.

Monodromy construction scheme

Other crucial constraint that will appear more efficient for derivation of properties and algebra of models in the non-additive sector
5
,
6
,
7
– is the braiding algebra realised by intertwining the monodromy
T(u)
and the
R
-matrix,

R_{0\tilde{0}}(u-v)T_{0}(u)T_{\tilde{0}}(v)=T_{\tilde{0}}(v)T_{0}(u)R_{0\tilde{0}}(u-v)

(2.25)

where the monodromy
T_{0,\{1,\dots,L\}}(u)
acts on

physical spaces and one extra space is auxiliary, which we can set through the sequence of the Lax matrices
\mathcal{L}
as can noticed from Fig.

T_{m_{1}m_{2}\dots m_{L},\,\nu}^{n_{1}n_{2}\dots n_{L},\,\mu}=\mathcal{L}_{s_{2}m_{1}}^{\mu\,n_{1}}(u-\theta_{1})\mathcal{L}_{s_{3}m_{2}}^{s_{2}n_{2}}(u-\theta_{2})\dots\mathcal{L}_{\nu m_{L}}^{s_{L}n_{L}}(u-\theta_{L})

correspond to the space position free indices. The notion of
\mathcal{L}_{i,a}
as a linear operator involved in auxiliary spectral problem comes from resolution of integrable systems through Classical Inverse Scattering Method. Specifically,
\mathcal{L}
structure is defined on local quantum

and auxiliary

spaces, i.e. its action is defined on
\mathfrak{h}_{i}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{a}
. In the case of isotropic
